Title: Comprehensive Resolution of the Gauss-Fourier Conjecture for the First Kind of Zeros

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.30620v1

Published Time: Fri, 23 Oct 2026 00:31:23 GMT

Number of Pages: 89

Markdown Content:
COMPREHENSIVE RESOLUTION OF THE GAUSS-FOURIER CONJECTURE FOR THE FIRST KIND OF ZEROS 

LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA 

Abstract. For over two centuries, the esteemed mathematicians Gauss and Fourier conjectured that each real critical point of the rational function p′

(s)/p (s)is associated with a pair of non-real zeros of the real polynomial p(s). The conjecture remains unresolved. The real zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

that are real multiple zero of p′

(s) are defined as the second kind of zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

.In addition to the second kind of zeros, the other real zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

are referred to as the first kind of zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

. We demonstrate that the first kind of zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

emerge from the intersection of root loci emitted from non-real zeros of p(s), thereby providing a comprehensive geo-metric interpretation of their relationship. Moreover, each first kind of zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is connected via root loci to a pair of conjugate non-real ze-ros of p(s). This establishes the precise geometric relationship conjectured by Gauss and Fourier, fully resolving their original problem for first-kind zeros and completing a foundational chapter in polynomial theory. We have proven Gauss-Fourier conjecture about the first kind of zeros. 

Introduction 

In 2004, Borcea and Shapiro proposed a conjecture in their paper [14]. Their work originated from an attempt to prove the ’Hawaii Conjecture’ proposed by Craven, Csordas, and Smith [3]. The Hawaii Conjecture has its roots in the work of Gauss and Fourier. In the paper [22], Edwards and Hinkkanen presented a counterexample to the conjecture proposed by Borcea and Shapiro. They also provided an in-depth analysis of the work of Gauss and Fourier. The contributions of Gauss and Fourier have garnered considerable attention from the mathematical community. To accurately represent the work and history of Gauss and Fourier, we have directly quoted passages from [22]. ”In his correspondence, ”G¨ ottingische gelehrte Anzeigen” (G.G.A.) from Febru-ary 25, 1833 [1, 2], Gauss describes some observations and a conjecture of Fourier.” ”Gauss explains that Fourier believed that for each real zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

(or, as Fourier calls them, ”critical points”), there exists an associated pair of non-real zeros of the polynomial p(s). We will call the zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

Fourier critical points of p(s). The real Fourier critical points can be loosely associated with real inflection points of p(s). Suppose that the interval ( a, a ′

) does not contain any real zeros of p. Then, Rolle’s theorem implies that between any two real zeros of 

p′

(s) on ( a, a ′

), there is a real zero of p′′ 

(s), an inflection point. Further, on ( a, a ′

), the real zeros of p′

(s) are precisely the real zeros of p′

(s)/p (s), and again, Rolle’s

Key words and phrases. Gauss-Fourier Conjecture; non-real zeros; critical points; rational functions; root locus.

theorem gives a real zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

between the real zeros of p′

(s). Thus, there is a correspondence between some of the inflection points of p(s) and the real Fourier critical points. In this correspondence, Gauss notes that this association, occurring in a natural way and not involving arbitrary choices, of specific pairs of non-real zeros of p(s) to real Fourier critical points of p(s) is not proved and more investigation is needed. Later, in a correspondence with Schumacher on April 2, 1833 [2], Gauss explains that he did not comment on his belief or disbelief of Fourier’s ideas because he feels that the G.G.A. is not the appropriate place to do so [2]. A few years later, he wrote to Schumacher (June 20, 1836), stating that he believed that a connection could be made between the real Fourier critical points and certain pairs of non-real zeros of the polynomial. A few days later (June 24, 1836), however, Gauss changed his mind and wrote to Schumacher that upon further reflection, he has found, to the contrary, that a relationship between the individual real Fourier critical points and the individual pairs of non-real zeros of 

p(s) does not exist.” ”A restatement of the conjecture could be: Conjecture 3. Let p(s) be a real polynomial. Real zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

(Fourier critical points) can be grouped in pairs so that to each such pair there is an asso-ciated pair of complex conjugate zeros of p(s).” ”This conjecture changes the association from one real Fourier critical point and a pair of non-real zeros to the association between a pair of real Fourier critical points and a pair of non-real zeros. Note that with this conjecture, we can have more non-real zeros of p(s) than real Fourier critical points, but we may not have more real Fourier critical points than non-real zeros. Of course, it is intended that distinct pairs of non-real zeros be used for distinct pairs of real Fourier critical points, with obvious exceptions allowed in case there are multiple real Fourier critical points or multiple non-real zeros. So if all these points are simple, then to distinct pairs of real Fourier critical points we must associate distinct pairs of non-real zeros.” ”The Hawaii Conjecture is an attempt by Craven, Csordas, and Smith to quan-tify the ideas that Gauss and Fourier were investigating. The statement of their conjecture is that the number of real zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

does not exceed the num-ber of non-real zeros of p. They make no mention of an association between zeros. Gauss’s and Fourier’s attempts to find a specific association made their ideas and conjectures difficult to articulate precisely; however, it is evident from the corre-spondence that they were trying to understand exactly how these zeros and critical points were related. Both Gauss and Fourier seemed to express frustration about not being able to precisely state and prove these conjectures. The Hawaii Conjec-ture, a conjecture on merely counting numbers of zeros, is much weaker than what Gauss and Fourier were considering.” If all zeros of a real polynomial p(s) are simple real zeros, then the polynomial 

p(s) is (locally) strictly monotonic, as Gauss noted in [1, 2]. This can be expressed through the Laguerre inequality: if p(s) has only real zeros, then ( p′

)2 − pp ′′ 

≥ 0. The Laguerre-P´ olya class (LP class) consists of those entire functions that converge uniformly on compact sets to a polynomial sequence, have only real zeros, and these functions also satisfy the Laguerre inequality, as stated in [4]. The Wiman and P´ olya conjectures refine the Laguerre inequality. The Wiman conjecture was proved by Bergweiler, Eremenko, and Langley [5], while one of COMPREHENSIVE RESOLUTION OF THE GAUSS-FOURIER CONJECTURE FOR THE FIRST KIND OF ZEROS 3

P´ olya’s conjectures was settled by Craven, Csordas, and Smith [3], and later the other was settled by Bergweiler and Eremenko [6]. On the basis of these results, several conditions determine when a real entire function f (s) must belong to the LP-class, typically involving differential polyno-mials such as f (s)f ′′ 

(s) − κ(f ′

(s)) 2, where κ is a positive real number, see [7]. For a meromorphic function f (s), the zeros of f (s)f ′′ 

(s) − κ(f ′

(s)) 2 have been studied in [8, 9]. In 1989, Craven, Csordas, and Smith [3] proposed the Hawaii Conjecture while exploring the conjectures proposed by P´ olya[20, 21] and Wiman [23, 24]. The Hawaii Conjecture was proved in 2011 by Tyaglov [11]. Later B. Shapiro introduced three new conjectures around the Hawaii Conjecture, see Conjectures 11, 12 and 13 in [13]. In January 2024, for Shapiro’s Conjecture 12, our paper [15] proves the necessary and sufficient conditions for the conjecture to hold. Drawing from historical data compiled by various mathematicians, it is evident that a distinct branch of mathematics has evolved over the past two centuries, rooted in the conjectures of Gauss and Fourier. While subsequent conjectures have been successively resolved [20, 21, 23, 24, 3, 5, 6, 11, 13, 15], the original conjecture proposed by Gauss and Fourier remains unsolved. In fact, aside from the initial examples provided by these pioneers, no further positive results have been proved or discovered. Since the inception of complex analysis, mathematicians have consistently sought to understand the origins of critical points of complex functions. They aim to identify the factors within the original function that lead to the emergence of zeros in its derivative. As is widely recognized, in the two-dimensional real plane, a real curve has a tangent line parallel to the real axis. Therefore, the real function has a real critical point. The following question arises: How are the critical points of a general complex function formed? What governs these critical points of complex functions? This is a fundamental question mathematicians are eager to answer. This question predates the Gauss-Fourier conjecture and remains a crucial issue in fundamental mathematics, unresolved for over two centuries. Consequently, the Gauss-Fourier conjecture is likely a specific case of a broader problem. Fourier and Gauss sought to understand how real Fourier critical points are generated and geometrically linked to the non-real zeros of the polynomial p(s) in the complex plane. In other words, they sought to determine the geometric structure that connects these two categories of zeros in the complex plane. Moreover, the question of how critical points of a function are generated is highly important and arguably even more important than the Gauss-Fourier conjecture itself. Gauss and Fourier initially sought to uncover the principles governing the generation of critical points for general functions, and to find the geometric struc-ture that connects the real Fourier critical points and the non-real complex zeros of the polynomial p(s), leading them to propose this conjecture. In this paper, we present findings using the root locus theory for complex rational fractions. In the extended complex plane C ∪ {∞} , the set of points s where the argument of a rational function RF (s) is equal to α degrees forms a curve known as the root loci of α degree. The argument of RF (s) at each point on a specific curve has the same phase angle. The argument of RF (s) at a point on a certain 4 LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA 

root locus is α degree. The degrees of distinct curves are typically distinct. (The phase angle is the argument). α is the degree of the root locus. Notably, adjacent root loci generally have distinct degrees. As a rule, root loci with distinct unit complex numbers do not intersect. How-ever, an exception arises when the unit complex numbers are identical; under this condition, the root loci may intersect. In root locus theory, such intersection points are termed ”breakaway points.” A breakaway point of the root loci is a critical point of the complex rational fraction. Thus, we deduce the following: a critical point of a complex rational function must coincide with the intersection point of multiple root loci that share the same unit complex number value. If multiple root loci with the same unit complex number intersect at a single point, this point is identified as the critical point of the complex rational function. For all rational function functions, this condition is both necessary and sufficient. We have elucidated the mechanism behind the emergence of critical points in complex rational functions. These critical points arise from the intersection of multiple root loci that share identical unit complex numbers. The expression ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

or the first kind of zeros. 

The Gauss-Fourier conjecture is a conjecture about the critical points of rational functions. In our work, we have demonstrated the foundational principle behind the formation of critical points for a broad spectrum of complex rational functions. Furthermore, we have introduced a result that encompasses a broader scope than the Gauss-Fourier conjecture itself. Utilizing the root locus, we can provide the reason of the origination of the real Fourier critical points, as well as the geometric connections between real Fourier critical points and non-real zeros of the polynomial 

p(s). Therefore, we must use the results of root locus theory to address the Gauss-Fourier conjecture. The rational function p′

> p(s)

can form the root locus equation: K p′

= a + ib ,where K is called the gain. Based on the ideas of Gauss and Fourier and our root locus results, for the first kind of zeros, resolving the Gauss-Fourier conjecture requires addressing the following three conjectures. Conjecture 0.1. Only the root locus emitted from a non-real zero of the real poly-nomial p(s) intersects on the real axis. A first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

can be generated. A 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree root locus emitted from at least one pair of the non-real zeros of p intersects with other root loci on the real axis. On the real axis, the intersection point of the root loci is a first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

.

Conjecture 0.2. Each first kind zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is connected to a pair of the non-real zeros of the real polynomial p(s) through at least one 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree root locus emitted from this pair of non-real zeros. 

Conjecture 0.3. Each first kind zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is associated with a pair of non-real zeros of the real polynomial p(s).

Here, q = ±1, ±2, · · · is an integer, and the argument of the real value is 2 qπ or 2qπ + π.In this paper, we prove Theorem 0.3 and Theorem 0.4. 

Theorem 0.3. Only the root locus emitted from a non-real zero of the real poly-nomial p(s) intersects on the real axis. A first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

can be generated. A 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree root locus emitted from at least one pair of the non-real zeros of p(s) intersects with other root loci on the real axis. On the real axis, the intersection point of the root loci is a first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

.

Theorem 0.4. Each first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is connected to a pair of the non-real zeros of the real polynomial p(s) through at least one 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree root locus emitted from this pair of non-real zeros of p(s).

The relationship connecting the two types of points is one class of correlation relations. The second-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

must be connected with non-real zeros. Theorem 0.3 proves such a result. This result proves: each second-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is associated with a pair of non-real zeros of the real polynomial p(s). 

Theorem 0.5. For any real polynomial p(s), each first kind zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is necessarily associated with a pair of non-real zeros of p(s).

Using the root locus method, we demonstrate all possible connections in the com-plex plane between first-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

and the non-real complex zeros of the real polynomial p(s). For first kind of zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

, we establish its origination and geometric relationship desired by Fourier and Gauss. Conse-quently, we have achieved a complete resolution of the Gauss-Fourier conjecture for first-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

.In the context of associated relationships, the first type is the generating relation-ship. Theorem 0.3 proves that the emergence of each first-kind zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

necessarily involves a pair of non-real zeros. In the context of associated relationships, similarly, the second type is the con-nection relationship. Theorem 0.4 proves that each first-kind zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

must be connected to a pair of non-real zeros. Collectively, these two results demonstrate that each first-kind zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is associated with a pair of non-real zeros of the real polynomial p(s). In Example 2.31, we obtain two first-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

. These two first-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

are generated by the intersection of the root loci emitted 6 LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA 

from non-real zeros. Furthermore, through the intersecting root loci, these two first-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

are connected to the non-real zeros. However, the generation of the two first-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is not related to any real zeros of p(s), and at the same time, these two first-kind zeros cannot be connected to the real zeros of p(s). This example also demonstrates that each first-kind zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is associated with a pair of non-real zeros of the real polynomial p(s). In contrast, each first-kind zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

may not be associated with real zeros of the real polynomial p(s). 1. The proofs of the root locus method

Lemma 1.6 provides the sufficient condition for Theorem 1.8. Lemma 1.7 pro-vides the necessary condition for Theorem 1.8. By combining Lemma 1.6 and Lemma 1.7, we obtain Theorem 1.8. 

Theorem 1.8. Let s = σ + it ∈ ∆ be an arbitrary point. The point s lie on the locus of roots of α degree of (1.1) if and only if s satisfies (1.2). 

Definition 1.9. Let Ξ be the point set of all points on the locus of roots of equation (1.1) traced out in C ∪ {∞} when α = 2 qπ + arg( a + ib ) is a constant. The path of the roots of equation (1.1) in C ∪ {∞} is called the root locus of (1.1) . In other words, the set Ξ is the root locus of the α degree .When 2 qπ +arg (a + ib ) is a constant, the points in C∪{∞} that satisfy equation (1.2) form curves. These curves are the root loci of (1.1) and (1.2). Theorem 1.8 proves that the root locus of (1.1) is the same as the root locus of (1.2). Determining the root locus requires two elements. One is the unit complex value 

a + ib . Generally, distinct unit complex values a + ib lead to distinct root loci. The other is the points on the root locus. Points on the root locus can be determined via (1.1), and they are both sufficient and necessary to be determined via (1.2). Lemma 1.5 offers a method to calculate the degree of the root locus of (1.1) via the function φ(σ, t ). Lemma 1.11. Let z1 be a finite zero of (1.1) with exponent γ1. Then, z1 lies simultaneously on the root loci of all degrees of (1.1) from 2qπ degree to 2qπ + 2 γ1π

degree. Proof. Assume that the point z1 is an arbitrary finite zero of (1.1). When we substitute the point z1 into RF , we obtain   

> Qml=1 (z1−zl)γl

= 0. This equation can also be written in polar form as   

> Qml=1 (z1−zl)γl
> Qnj=1 (z1−pj)βj

= Kz1 eiθ z1 = 0. Kz1 is the modulus of RF and θz1 is the argument of RF . Since the product is zero, we have Kz1 = 0. Then Kz1 ei(θz1 ) = 0 · (cos (θz1 ) + isin (θz1 )) = 0 holds for any value of θz1 . Here, θz1

can take any value from the range 2 qπ degree to 2 qπ + 2 γ1π degree. For the point z1 which makes the left-hand side of (1.1) equal to 0, regardless of the value of the argument θz1 . cos (θz1 ) + isin (θz1 ) is the non-zero and finite, and the modulus of the factor cos (θz1 ) + isin (θz1 ) is 1. θz1 represents an arbitrary degree number, which shows the following: no matter what value θz1 is, because the modulus Kz1 is 0, there is Kz1 ei(θz1 ) = 0 · (cos( θz1 ) + i sin( θz1 )) = 0. When a finite zero z1 is substituted into RF , for any argument θz1 of RF with degree number in the given range, the function values of z1 are all equal to 0. Since θz1 can take all values from 2 qπ degree to 2 qπ + 2 γ1π degree, this implies that finite zeros of (1.1) lie simultaneously on the root loci of all degree numbers of (1.1) from 2 qπ degree to 2 qπ + 2 γ1π degree. □

Lemma 1.12. Let p1 be a finite pole of (1.1) with exponent β1. Then, p1 lies simultaneously on the root loci of all degrees of (1.1) from 2qπ degree to 2qπ + 2 β1π

= ∞.In complex analysis, 0 can be written as: 0 ·eiθ = 0 ·(cos θ +i sin θ). The non-zero finite values can also be written as: k · eiθ = k · (cos θ + i sin θ). Infinity can also be written as: (+ ∞) · eiθ = (+ ∞) · (cos θ + i sin θ). In which, (cos θ + i sin θ) is a non-zero and infinite, and its modulus is 1 of the unit complex number value. For (+ ∞) · eiθ = (+ ∞) · (cos θ + i sin θ), no matter what value θ is obtained on the unit circle in C ∪ {∞} , the values of this expression all obtain the infinity. 10 LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA

Lemma 1.13. n finite poles of (1.1) are n origination points of the root locus of any α degree of (1.1). m finite zeros of (1.1) are m ending points of the root locus of any α degree of (1.1). Proof. The root locus of any α degree of (1.1) consists of curves in C ∪ {∞} that must have their own origination and ending points. On the basis of the relationship between the points in C∪{∞} and the root locus of (1.1), we can classify the points in C ∪ {∞} into four types: n finite poles of (1.1), m finite zeros of (1.1), general finite points on the root locus of α degree of (1.1), and the infinity point. Except for n finite poles and m finite zeros of (1.1), a general finite point in 

C ∪ {∞} lies on the root locus of a specific degree number of (1.1). These general points only have a phase angle and a non-zero finite gain value, so they are not suitable as the origination or ending points of the root locus of (1.1). 

n finite poles and m finite zeros of (1.1) lie on the root loci of all degree numbers of (1.1). Since an infinite number of root locus curves of distinct degree numbers intersect at the same point, they meet the requirements for being the origination or ending points of the root locus. Moreover, for n finite poles of (1.1), the gain 

K = 0, which means that they act as origination points. For m finite zeros of (1.1), the gain K = + ∞, which means that they act as ending points of the root locus. The result of the infinity point is in Lemma 1.3. When (1) in Lemma 1.3 holds, the point at infinity s∞ is an origination point of the root locus of (1.1). When (3) in Lemma 1.3 holds, the point at infinity s∞ is a ending point of the root locus of (1.1). □

For a point s1 = σ1 + it 1 on a root locus, substituting s1 into the rational func-tion RF (s1) yields a complex value u(σ1, t 1) + iv (σ1, t 1) of RF (s1). The argument 

arg (u(σ1, t 1) + iv (σ1, t 1)) of RF (s1) is referred to as the phase angle, which repre-sents the degree of the root locus of (1.1). Each degree of the root locus of (1.1) is the degree value of an angle. Each degree of the root locus corresponds to a unique unit complex value. Consequently, each root locus is characterized by its degree value and its unit complex value. Root loci with distinct degrees may share COMPREHENSIVE RESOLUTION OF THE GAUSS-FOURIER CONJECTURE FOR THE FIRST KIND OF ZEROS 11 

the same unit complex value, and the difference in their degrees is 2 qπ . The unit complex value of the root locus is the unit complex value of the degree of the root locus of (1.1). 

Theorem 1.15. Two root loci of (1.1) with distinct unit complex values a1 + ib 1

and a2 + ib 2 cannot intersect in C ∪ {∞} .Proof. Assume that two root loci of (1.1) with distinct unit complex values a1 + ib 1

and a2 + ib 2 intersect at a point s0, which is neither a zero nor a pole of (1.1). Since s0 is the same point, its gain K0 should be the same. s0 allows the following two equations to hold simultaneously. K0  

> Qml=1 (s0−zl)γl
> Qnj=1 (s0−pj)βj

= a1 + ib 1, and 

K0  

> Qml=1 (s0−zl)γl
> Qnj=1 (s0−pj)βj

= a2+ib 2. Subtracting these equations yields ( a1−a2)+ i(b1−b2) = 0. By the conditions of this theorem, a1̸ = a2 or b1̸ = b2, so this equation cannot hold. Since the contradiction arises from the assumption that the two root loci intersect at s0, this assumption is false. □

Theorem 1.16. Two root loci of (1.1) with identical unit complex values a + ib 

may intersect in C ∪ {∞} .Proof. Consider the root locus equation K 

> (s−1)( s−3)

= a + ib . The interval [1 , 3] contains root loci of 2 qπ + π degree from both poles at s = 1 and s = 3, each with a unit complex value of −1. These root loci intersect at point s = 2. By applying Rolle’s theorem, there exists a critical point of the function ( s −

1)( s − 3) in the interval [1 , 3]. This critical point of ( s − 1)( s − 3) is s = 2, which is the intersection point of the root loci. □

Lemma 1.17. All points in C ∪ {∞} are roots of the root locus equation (1.1). Except for zeros and poles of (1.1), each finite point in C ∪ {∞} lies on a root locus of (1.1). | yields a positive, finite real number. Similarly, substituting fa into the phase angle expression φ(σ, t ) yields a finite certain phase angle φ(σa, t a). Lemma 1.23 proves the sufficient condition for breakaway points, whereas Lemma 1.24 proves the necessary condition. Combining these two lemmas, we obtain the following sufficient and necessary conditions for breakaway points. 

Theorem 1.25. Let s0 = ( σ0 + it 0) ∈ ∆. The finite point s0 is a finite breakaway point of the root loci of (1.1) if and only if s0 satisfies (1.4). 

When the unit complex values of the root loci are distinct, these root loci cannot intersect. When the unit complex values of the root loci are identical, (under these conditions)the root loci may intersect. The intersection points at finite locations are finite breakaway points. These intersected root loci separate from these finite breakaway points again. Multiple zeros and poles of the root locus equation cannot exhibit this property. Thus, the finite breakaway points of the root loci are distinct from the multiple zeros and poles of (1.1).

By Theorem 1.25, the intersection points of the root loci of (1.1) are critical points of RF (s). 

Lemma 1.27. Assume RF is a real constant coefficient rational function. Then: (1). The root loci of 2qπ and 2qπ + π degree of (1.1) are symmetric about the real axis. (2). The root loci on the real axis are the root loci of 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree of (1.1). (3). An interval on the real axis is a 2qπ + π degree root locus if and only if the number of real poles and zeros of (1.1) to its right is odd. (4). An interval on the real axis is a 2qπ degree root locus if and only if the number of real poles and zeros of (1.1) to its right is even. (5). The interval between two adjacent zero and pole (zero-pole, zero-zero, or pole-pole) is the root loci of the same degree. Proof. (1). The rational function can be written as RF (s) = u(σ, t )+ iv (σ, t ). Since 

RF is a real constant coefficient rational function, it is a conjugate function. For the conjugate function RF , RF (s) = u(σ, t ) − iv (σ, t ). For the root loci of 2 qπ and 2qπ + π degree of (1.1), v(σ, t ) = 0. Therefore, for all points on these root loci of 2qπ and 2 qπ + π degree of (1.1), RF (s) = RF (s) = u(σ, t ). Thus, the root loci of 2qπ and 2 qπ + π degree of (1.1) are symmetric about the real axis. (2). Since RF is a real constant coefficient rational function, it takes real values on the real axis. Hence, the root loci on the real axis are the root loci of 2 qπ and 2qπ + π degree of (1.1). For any point s0 on the selected section of the real axis, the zeros and poles of (1.1) are conjugate symmetric with respect to the real axis. The sum of the phase angles of the vectors between conjugate complex numbers and an arbitrary point on the real axis is 2 π. Therefore, determining the phase angle of an arbitrary point on the real axis does not require considering the effects of conjugate zeros and poles. When determining the degree of the root locus of a real constant coefficient rational function on the real axis, we do not need to consider the conjugate zeros and poles. 16 LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA 

The phase angle of vector between a real zero on the left side of s0 and point 

s0 is 2 π. The phase angle of vector between a real pole on the left side of s0 and point s0 is 2 π. Thus, determining the phase angle of an arbitrary point on the real axis does not need to consider the real zeros and real poles on the left side of s0.When determining the degree of the root locus of a real constant coefficient rational function on the real axis, we do not need to consider the real zeros and real poles on the left side of s0.Let zl be a real zero to the right of s0, and let γl be the exponent of zl. Theorem 1.25 provides a necessary and sufficient condition for breakaway points. Theorems 1.25 and 1.26 together explain the mechanism of emergence of critical points in complex rational functions. When root loci have distinct unit complex values, they cannot intersect. When root loci share the same unit complex value, they can intersect. Such intersection points are called ”breakaway points” in root locus theory, and each breakaway point is a critical point of the complex rational function. These critical points occur at the intersection points of multiple root loci that share identical unit complex values. By Lemma 2.2, all infinite points in C ∪ {∞} are zeros of (2.2), which can only receive root loci but cannot emit the root loci. In C ∪ {∞} , no other poles can emit root loci. Therefore, each zero of p′

(s) receives the root loci that are emitted exclusively from the zeros of p(s). Thus: 

Lemma 2.4. Each zero of the root locus equation (2.2) receives root loci, which can only be emitted from the zeros of the real polynomial p(s).

The real zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

are classified into two kinds. Those that are real multiple zeros of p′

(s) constitute the second kind of zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

. The remaining real zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

are referred to as the first kind of zeros of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

. In fact, the first kind of zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

are the intersection points of the root loci of (2.2). In the subsequent proof, the concept of root locus extension is essential, it means a root locus moves from a point closer to a pole to a point closer to a zero. Demonstrating that the generation of real zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

relates to non-real zeros of p(s) constitutes the most crucial result for their correlation. This is precisely the result Gauss and Fourier sought. Moreover, revealing the principle behind critical point generation in functions holds greater significance than the Gauss-Fourier conjecture itself. 

Theorem 2.5. Suppose the real polynomial p(s) has at least one real zero. Then, each real pole of (2.2) (i.e., real zero of p(s)) emits exactly one root locus of 2qπ 

degree to one side (left or right) and one root locus of 2qπ + π degree to the opposite COMPREHENSIVE RESOLUTION OF THE GAUSS-FOURIER CONJECTURE FOR THE FIRST KIND OF ZEROS 21 

side. No additional root loci of 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree are emitted from real poles, and no root loci of 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree enter C∗ directly from real poles. Proof. By Theorem 1.27, the real axis is its root loci of 2 qπ and 2 qπ + π degrees of (2.2). By Lemma 2.1, all poles of (2.2) are simple. By Theorem 1.35, each real pole of (2.2) (i.e., real zero of p(s)) emits exactly one root locus of 2 qπ degree and one root locus of 2 qπ + π degree . By Theorem 1.27 and Theorem 1.31, each real pole of (2.2) only emits one root locus of 2 qπ degree and one root locus of 2 qπ + π

degree to real axis. Therefore, each real pole of (2.2) (i.e., real zero of p(s)) emits exactly one root locus of 2 qπ degree to one side (left or right) and one root locus of 2 qπ + π degree to the opposite side. No additional root loci of 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree are emitted from real poles, and no root loci of 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree enter C∗ directly from real poles. □

Theorem 2.6. Suppose the real polynomial p(s) has at least one real zero. Assume that the root loci emitted from the non-real zeros of p(s) do not intersect the real axis. Then, the root loci emitted from the real zeros of p(s) cannot leave the real axis and enter C∗. The first kind of zeros of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

cannot be generated. Proof. By Lemma 2.3, real zeros of (2.2) exist on both sides of each real pole. Thus, the 2 qπ degree and 2 qπ + π degree root loci emitted from each real pole of (2.2) reach the adjacent real zeros of (2.2)(which may include zeros at infinity). By Lemma 2.4, each zero of p′

(s) receives root loci emitted exclusively from zeros of p(s). By Theorem 2.5, each real zero of p(s) emits only one 2 qπ degree root locus and one 2 qπ + π degree root locus to the real axis. Assume that root loci of 2 qπ and 2 qπ + π degree emitted from non-real zeros of p(s) do not intersect the real axis, the root loci of 2 qπ and 2 qπ + π degree emitted from real zeros of p(s)directly reach the adjacent real zeros of (2.2) on the real axis. cannot be generated. □

By Theorem 1.25 and Theorem 1.26, the real breakaway points of (2.2) are first kind of zeros of ( p′

. So, by Lemma 2.8, we have Corollary 2.1. 

Corollary 2.1. If there exists at least a first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

, then the real polynomial p(s) must have at least one pair of the non-real zeros. 

Lemma 2.9. Suppose p(s) is a real polynomial with no real zeros. Only the root locus emitted from a non-real zero of the real polynomial p(s) intersects on the real axis. A first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

can be generated. A 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree root locus emitted from at least one pair of the non-real zeros of p(s) intersects with 22 LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA 

other root loci on the real axis. On the real axis, the intersection point of the root loci is a first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

.Proof. Since p(s) has no real zeros and is a real polynomial of even degree, its derivative p′

(s) is a real polynomial of odd degree and therefore has at least one real zero. On the real axis (including the two zeros at infinity of (2.2)), equation (2.2) has at least three real zeros. However, there are no real poles of (2.2) on the real axis. Thus, the real zeros of (2.2) partition the real axis into several intervals. The rational function p′(s)/p (s) is real-valued on the real axis, and equation (2.2) is a real root locus equation. By Theorem 1.27, the real axis consists of root loci of (2.2) with 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree. Since (2.2) has no real poles, Lemma 1.13 implies that root loci can only originate from non-real poles of (2.2). As all poles of (2.2) lie in C∗ (none on the real axis), the root loci with 2 qπ and 2 qπ + π degree emitted from these non-real poles must enter the real axis. All real zeros of (2.2) must receive root loci with 2 qπ and 2 qπ + π degree. By Lemma 2.4, each zero of p′

(s) receives root loci that can only be emitted from zeros of p(s). Specifically, each real zero of p′

(s) receives root loci on the real axis that are emitted from non-real zeros of p(s). Furthermore, there exist root loci with 2 qπ 

and 2 qπ + π degree emitted from conjugate non-real zeros of p(s); these root loci are symmetric about the real axis and must enter the real axis, terminating at real zeros of (2.2). 1. Due to the symmetry of the root loci of (2.2) about the real axis, root loci must intersect at a point on the real axis before entering it. 2. When two distinct root loci meet on the real axis, they must intersect at a point. Both scenarios generate real breakaway points. 3. There is another way to generate real breakaway points. The root loci emitted from non-real poles do not enter the real axis. They only intersect at real breakaway points, and after the intersection, these root loci directly enter C∗. In this case, the same root locus lies on both sides of the real axis at the real breakaway point. There are the only three ways real breakaway points are generated. Since (2.2) has no poles on the real axis, all its root loci originate from non-real poles. Theorem 1.26 establishes that critical points of RF (s) are intersection points of root loci of (1.1). Thus, each first-kind zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

corresponds to an intersection of root loci of (2.2) on the real axis. Therefore, among these three ways of generating real breakaway points, all real breakaway points of (2.2) are generated by the intersections of root loci emitted from non-real zeros of p(s), and these real breakaway points are precisely the first-kind zeros of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

.Consequently, only the root locus emitted from a non-real zero of the real poly-nomial p(s) intersects on the real axis. A first kind of zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

can be generated. A 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree root locus emitted from at least one pair of the non-real zeros of p(s) intersects with other root loci on the real axis. On the real axis, the intersection point of the root loci is a first kind of zero of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

. □

Remark 2.10 . In the three generation ways of the real breakaway points in the proof of Lemma 2.9, there may arise a situation where the root loci from C∗ intersect at real breakaway points, and then these root loci from C∗ leave the real breakaway points and enter C∗. These root loci that intersect at real breakaway points meet at points on the real axis but do not enter the real axis. We may refer to this method COMPREHENSIVE RESOLUTION OF THE GAUSS-FOURIER CONJECTURE FOR THE FIRST KIND OF ZEROS 23 

as the third method. The results and proofs associated with the third method are exactly the same as those of the previous two methods. Therefore, in the following results and proofs, we will omit any further mention of the third method and will not provide its results or proofs separately. 

Lemma 2.11. Suppose the real polynomial p(s) has at least one real zero. For root loci intersecting at a real breakaway point Qa in (2.2), if at least one of them originates directly from a non-real zero sa or sa of p(s), this root locus emitted from non-real zero sa or sa of p(s) intersects with other root loci at Qa on the real axis. Then, the intersection point Qa of the root loci is a first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

.Proof. Consider root loci intersecting at a real breakaway point Qa in (2.2) where at least one root locus comes directly from a non-real zero sa or sa of p(s). The real breakaway points formed under this condition constitute the first-kind zeros of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

. Since the intersecting root loci contain the root locus coming from a non-real zero sa or sa of p(s), the first kind of zero Qa of ( p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

is generated by the intersection of root locus of the conjugate non-real zeros sa or sa of p(s). Therefore, at least one pair of the non-real zeros sa or sa of p(s) emits 2 qπ or 2qπ + π degree root locus, this root locus intersects with other root loci at Qa on the real axis. The intersection point Qa of the root loci is a first kind of zero of (p′

(s)/p (s)) ′

. □

With the exception of those intersecting at the real breakaway point Qk, 1, only those root loci that intersect gk,j 1,1 on the real axis can be considered as additional root loci. According to our previous results, when a pair of non-real conjugate zeros of p(s)emit root loci that intersect on the real axis, one of these root loci will intersect with the root locus emitted from a real zero. The root locus emitted from this real zero then leaves the real axis and later intersects with the root locus emitted from another real zero on the real axis. This process continues until the root locus finally reaches a zero of (2.2). This chain of intersections among root loci from non-real and real zeros constitutes the necessary condition for forming a standard linked root loci. The condition for the generation of the standard linked root loci is that two root loci emitted from non-real zeros and several root loci emitted from real zeros intersect on the real axis. A key question is under what circumstances a standard linked root loci must exist, particularly for the case studied in this paper. Our earlier results show that if all root loci intersecting to form the real breakaway point Qk, 1 originate from real zeros of p(s), then at least one root locus from a real zero may form a standard linked root loci with other root loci. This result ensures that the conclusions in the remainder of this paper are valid. Thus, for g2,1,1 to leave the real axis and enter C∗, another root locus must intersect g2,1,1 within L2,1,1. additional root loci does not exist. In the second kind of non-standard linked root loci, only the second type of intersection with the additional root loci occurs; the first type of intersection with the additional root loci does not exist. The two main lines of the second non-standard linked root loci are identical to the two main lines of the standard linked root loci. This outcome arises for a specific reason. The third kind of non-standard linked root loci must satisfy six conditions. The first condition: Two root loci gk,j 1+1 ,4 and gk,j 1+2 ,4 emitted from the real zeros ( tk,j 1+1 ,4, tk,j 1+2 ,4). The real zero tk,j 1,4 emits a root locus gk,j 1,4. Two root COMPREHENSIVE RESOLUTION OF THE GAUSS-FOURIER CONJECTURE FOR THE FIRST KIND OF ZEROS 77 

loci gk,k +1 ,4 and gk,k +1 ,4 emitted from the non-real zeros ( sk,k +1 ,4 or sk,k +1 ,4). The real zero tk,k, 4 emits a root locus gk,k, 4.The second condition: The two root loci gk,j 1+1 ,4 and gk,j 1+2 ,4 must intersect at the real breakaway point qk, 2j1−1,4 on the real axis. From qk, 2j1−1,4, the two root loci gk,j 1+1 ,4 and gk,j 1+2 ,4 extend further along the real axis in opposite directions. The two root loci gk,k +1 ,4 and gk,k +1 ,4 must intersect at the real breakaway point 

qk, 2k−1,4 on the real axis. From qk, 2k−1,4, the two root loci gk,k +1 ,4 and gk,k +1 ,4

extend further along the real axis in opposite directions. The preceding sentence states a condition, while the following sentence describes a property shared by all pairs of root loci intersecting on the real axis, rather than constituting an additional condition. The third condition: The root locus gk,j 1+1 ,4 further intersects with the addi-tional root loci on the real axis. The root locus gk,k +1 ,4 further intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis. The fourth condition: After gk,j 1+1 ,4 intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis, it then intersects with gk,j 1,4 on the real axis at the real breakaway point qk, 2j1−2,4. After gk,k +1 ,4 intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis, it then intersects with gk,k, 4 on the real axis at the real breakaway point 

qk, 2k−2,4.The fifth condition: The real zero tk,j 1,4 emits a root locus gk,j 1,4. This root locus gk,j 1,4 intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis. The sixth condition: After gk,j 1,4 intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis, it then intersects with gk,j 1+1 ,4 on the real axis at the real breakaway point qk, 2j1−2. After gk,k, 4 intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis, it then intersects with the root locus gk,k +1 ,4 on the real axis at the real breakaway point qk, 2k−2,4.The root loci satisfying these six conditions constitute the third kind of non-standard linked root loci. After the root locus gk,j 1+1 ,4 intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis, it may then intersect with a root locus gk,j 1,4 that does not intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis. This is one scenario. Alternatively, the root locus gk,j 1+1 ,4 may not intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis but may intersect with a root locus gk,j 1,4 that does intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis. This is another scenario. It is also possible that after gk,j 1+1 ,4

intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis, it then intersects with a root locus gk,j 1,4 that also intersects with the additional root loci on the real axis. This is yet another scenario. Finally, the root locus gk,j 1+1 ,4 may not intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis and may intersect with a root locus gk,j 1,4

that also does not intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis. This constitutes a further scenario. In the third kind of non-standard linked root loci, at least one instance of the first type of intersection with the additional root loci and at least one instance of the second type of intersection with the additional root loci must exist. This is precisely what distinguishes the third kind of non-standard linked root loci from the first and second kinds. The two main lines of the first kind of non-standard linked root loci are identical to the two main lines of the third kind of non-standard linked root loci. This outcome arises for a specific reason. 78 LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA

In the third kind of non-standard linked root loci, the root locus gk,j 1,4 emitted from the real zero tk,j 1,4. gk,j 1,4 starts from tk,j 1,4 and extends to the real breakaway point qk, 2j1−2,4. During its extension, it may intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis. Although the root locus gk,j 1,4 may intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis, the direction of its extension remains from tk,j 1,4 to the real breakaway point qk, 2j2−2,4. According to this direction of extension, this portion constitutes a branch of the third kind of non-standard linked root loci. It cannot lie on the two main lines of the third kind of non-standard linked root loci. 80 LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA 

In the third kind of non-standard linked root loci, the root locus gk,k, 4 emitted from the real zero tk,k, 4. It starts from tk,k, 4 and extends to the real breakaway point qk, 2k−2,4. During its extension, it may intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis. Although the root locus gk,k, 4 may intersect with the additional root loci on the real axis, the direction of its extension remains from tk,k, 4 to the real breakaway point qk, 2k−2,4. According to this direction of extension, this portion constitutes a branch of the third kind of non-standard linked root loci. It cannot lie on the two main lines of the third kind of non-standard linked root loci. Therefore, this demonstrates that the two main lines of the first kind of non-standard linked root loci are identical to the two main lines of the third kind of non-standard linked root loci. Based on the previously presented results for linked root loci, there can only be these four scenarios for generating linked root loci. The first scenario: In the linked root loci, there exist neither the first type nor the second type of intersection with the additional root loci. The linked root loci are the standard linked root loci. The second scenario: In the linked root loci, only the first type of intersection with the additional root loci exists; the second type does not exist. The linked root loci are the first kind of non-standard linked root loci. The third scenario: In the linked root loci, only the second type of intersection with the additional root loci exists; the first type does not exist. The linked root loci are the second kind of non-standard linked root loci. The fourth scenario: In the linked root loci, both the first type and the second type of intersection with the additional root loci exist. The linked root loci are the third kind of non-standard linked root loci. 

Definition 2.44. Standard linked root loci, the first kind of non-standard linked root loci, the second kind of non-standard linked root loci, and the third kind of non-standard linked root loci together constitute the linked root loci. 

Definition 2.45. The non-real zeros sk,k +1 ,j 3 and sk,k +1 ,j 3 emit root loci gk,k +1 ,j 3

and gk,k +1 ,j 3 . In the linked root loci, beginning at the non-real zeros sk,k +1 ,j 3 and 

sk,k +1 ,j 3 , the two lines which gk,k +1 ,j 3 and gk,k +1 ,j 3 can extend all the way to Qk,j 3

are the two main lines of the linked root loci of (2.2). j3 = 1 , 2, 3, 4. For each of these four scenarios, two lemmas are provided. They are respectively: Lemma 2.17 and Lemma 2.19; Lemma 2.24 and Lemma 2.28; Lemma 2.33 and Lemma 2.35; Lemma 2.39 and Lemma 2.43. Lemma 2.17, Lemma 2.24, Lemma 2.33, and Lemma 2.39 can be combined into Lemma 2.46. Lemma 2.19, Lemma 2.28, Lemma 2.35, and Lemma 2.43 can be combined into Lemma 2.47. This yields the two merged lemmas. 

Lemma 2.46. When a root locus gk, 1,j 3 emitted from a real zero of p(s) leaves the real axis and enters C∗, then, there must exist a pair of non-real zeros and k − 1

other real zeros. The root loci from the non-real zeros are gk,k +1 ,j 3 and gk,k +1 ,j 3 ,and root loci from the other real zeros are gk,j 1,j 3 (for 1 ≤ k < n , j1 = 1 , · · · , k ,

0 < 2j1 − 2 < 2k − 2, 0 < 2j1 − 1 < 2k − 1, j3 = 1 , 2, 3, 4). These root loci 

gk,j 1,j 3 , gk,k +1 ,j 3 and gk,k +1 ,j 3 intersect at real breakaway points Qk,j 3 , qk, 2j1−2,j 3 ,

qk, 2j1−1,j 3 on the real axis. These real breakaway points Qk,j 3 , qk, 2j1−2,j 3 , qk, 2j1−1,j 3

connect gk,j 1,j 3 , gk,k +1 ,j 3 , and gk,k +1 ,j 3 together, forming a linked root locus. The terminus of this structure consists of gk,k +1 ,j 3 and gk,k +1 ,j 3 .COMPREHENSIVE RESOLUTION OF THE GAUSS-FOURIER CONJECTURE FOR THE FIRST KIND OF ZEROS 81 

Lemma 2.47. In the linked root loci, a root locus gk,k +1 ,j 3 or gk,k +1 ,j 3 originating from a non-real zero sk,k +1 ,j 3 or sk,k +1 ,j 3 can be distributed on two main lines of the linked root loci. The root locus gk,k +1 ,j 3 or gk,k +1 ,j 3 intersects at the real breakaway point Qk,j 3 . The root locus gk,k +1 ,j 3 or gk,k +1 ,j 3 leaves from Qk,j 3 then enters C∗.(for 1 ≤ k < n , j1 = 1 , · · · , k , 0 < 2j1 − 2 < 2k − 2, 0 < 2j1 − 1 < 2k − 1,

j3 = 1 , 2, 3, 4). 

When all root loci intersecting at a real breakaway point of (2.2) originate directly from real zeros of p, the real breakaway points are generated in two distinct ways, classified as follows: (1) First category of real breakaway points: At least two root loci in-tersect at a real breakaway point Qa in an interval. One root locus comes from a real zero of p(s) at an endpoint of the interval. (1). If this root locus comes from a real zero of p(s) at an endpoint of the interval doesn’t intersect with the other root loci on the real axis and it remains on the real axis before intersecting the root loci originated from real zeros at Qa. This root locus extends along the real axis and intersect with the root loci from real zeros of p(s), generating a real breakaway point 

Qa.(2). If this root locus comes from a real zero of p(s) at an endpoint of the interval intersects with the other root loci on the real axis, this situation is like as the second type of the additional root loci intersection, after this root locus intersects with the other root loci, this root locus extends along the real axis and intersect with the root loci from real zeros of p(s), generating a real breakaway point Qa.(2) Second category of real breakaway points: All root loci generating the real breakaway point Qa originate from real zeros of p(s) but leave the real axis and enter C∗. Among these root loci which originate from real zeros of p(s), like first category of real breakaway points, there possibly exist the situation like as the second type of the additional root loci intersection. The root loci that originate from the real zero of p(s) must leave the real axis, and enter C∗. The root loci that originate from the real zeros of p(s)then intersect at Qa on the real axis. Here, the other root loci are not the additional root loci. For the first category of real breakaway points, a root locus emitted from an interval endpoint may first intersect with the other root loci and subsequently intersect at Qa. In fact, for both subcases, regardless of which subcase the real breakaway point Qa belongs to, it can be proven to be associated with at least one pair of non-real zeros of the polynomial p(s). Since p has at least one real zero, Lemma 2.3 implies that each real pole of (2.2) is separated by at least one real zero of p′

. Consequently, no two real poles of (2.2) are adjacent. Therefore, on the real axis, intervals exist between adjacent real zeros and real poles of (2.2), and may exist between adjacent real zeros, but not between adjacent real poles.

. □

Combining Lemmas 2.48 and 2.49 yields Lemma 2.50.

Lemma 2.51. Suppose the real polynomial p has at least one real zero. When the root loci intersect at the real breakaway point Qa. Then, at least one pair of the non-real zeros of p emits a 2qπ or 2qπ +π degree root locus, this root locus intersects with other root loci at Qa on the real axis. On the real axis, the intersection point 

Qa of the root loci is a first kind of zero of (p′

/p )′

.

The real breakaway point Qa is any real breakaway point. This real breakaway point can be produced by the intersection of root loci from non-real zeros. It can also be entirely generated by the intersection of root loci from real zeros. However, Lemma 2.46 proves that the root loci from real zeros and those of non-real zeros form linked root loci. Lemma 2.47 proves that the root locus from a non-real zero can be distributed on two main lines of the linked root loci. Therefore, this type of real breakaway point is also produced by the intersection of root loci from non-real zeros with other root loci. Thus, by Theorem 2.6 and Lemma 2.51, we have: 

Lemma 2.52. Suppose the real polynomial p has at least one real zero. Only the root locus emitted from a non-real zero of the real polynomial p intersects on the real axis. A first kind of zero of (p′

/p )′

can be generated. A 2qπ or 2qπ + π degree root locus emitted from at least one pair of the non-real zeros of p intersects with COMPREHENSIVE RESOLUTION OF THE GAUSS-FOURIER CONJECTURE FOR THE FIRST KIND OF ZEROS 85 

other root loci on the real axis. On the real axis, the intersection point of the root loci is a first kind of zero of (p′

/p )′

.

Lemma 2.9 proves the result which the real polynomial p has no any real zeros. Lemma 2.52 proves the result which the real polynomial p has at least one real zero. Lemmas 2.9 and 2.52 together imply Theorem 0.3. The proof of Theorem 0.4. 

Proof. In Theorem 0.3, at least one pair of the non-real zeros of p emits 2 qπ or 2qπ + π degree root locus, and this root locus intersects with other root loci on the real axis. Through the 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree root locus emitted from the pair of the non-real zeros of p, each first kind of zero of ( p′

/p )′

is connected to a pair of the non-real zeros of the real polynomial p. When we prove Theorem 0.3, we use three lemmas. They are Lemma 2.9, Lemma 2.11, and Lemma 2.52, respectively. In Lemma 2.9, only the root loci of non-real zeros generate first kind of zero of (p′

/p )′

. So, through the 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree root locus emitted from a pair of the non-real zeros of p, each first kind of zero of ( p′

/p )′

is connected to a pair of the non-real zeros of p.In Lemma 2.11, for root loci intersecting at a real breakaway point Qa in (2.2), if at least one of them originates directly from a non-real zero sa or sa of p, thus, through the 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree root locus emitted from a pair of the non-real zeros sa or sa of p, the first kind of zero Qa of ( p′

/p )′

is connected to a pair of the non-real zeros sa or sa of p.In Lemma 2.52, for root loci intersecting at a real breakaway point Qa in (2.2), if all of them originate directly from real zeros sk,j 1,j 3 of p. At least root locus from real zero of p forms a linked root loci. Through two main lines of the linked root loci, the first kind of zero Qa of ( p′

/p )′

is connected to a pair of the non-real zeros 

sk,k +1 ,j 3 or sk,k +1 ,j 3 of p.The first kind of zero Qa of ( p′

/p )′

is any real Fourier critical point. Therefore, we prove: through at least one 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree root locus of the non-real zeros of p, each first kind of zero of ( p′

/p )′

is connected to a pair of the non-real zeros of p. □

In fact, Lemma 2.52 has a dual nature. Its condition requires that all root loci intersecting at a real breakaway point Qa in (2.2) originate directly from real zeros of p. However, the lemma concludes that the 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree root locus emitted from at least one pair of non-real zeros of p intersects with other root loci at 

Qa on the real axis. While the root loci intersecting at Qa can originate exclusively from real zeros, root loci emitted from non-real zeros can also intersect with other root loci at Qa. Both scenarios are reasonable. Crucially, if terminal root loci from non-real zeros do not intersect on the real axis, then the scenario where all root loci originate from real zeros and intersect at Qa cannot exist. Therefore, we only show Lemma 2.52. We can give another result which the real Fourier critical point is generated: Now suppose the real polynomial p has at least one real zero. If all root loci intersecting at a real breakaway point Qa in (2.2) originate directly from real zeros of p, then at least one root locus starting from a real zero of p becomes the beginning root locus of the linked root loci, while the terminal root locus is emitted from a non-real zero. Under the condition that the root locus from a non-real zero intersects the real axis, the 2 qπ or 2 qπ + π degree root locus emitted from non-real zeros of 86 LANDE MA AND ZHAOKUN MA 

p intersects at Qa on the real axis. Here, the intersection point Qa is a first-kind zero of ( p′

/p )′

on the real axis. Under this condition, the root locus emitted from real zeros can generate real Fourier critical points. An alternative result on generating real Fourier critical points Qa presents this principle more comprehensively. Both Lemma 2.52 and this alternative result are accurate and reasonable. Lemma 2.52 is fully consistent with Lemma 2.9, whereas the alternative result is not. Therefore, we present only Lemma 2.52 and omit the alternative result on generating real Fourier critical points 

Qa.
